NEST : Network Enforced Session Types (Technical Report)
Jens Kanstrup Larsen, Alceste Scalas, Guy Amir, Jules Jacobs, Jana Wagemaker, Nate Foster

Abstract
This paper introduces NEST (Network-Enforced Session Types), a runtime verification framework that moves application-level protocol monitoring into the network fabric. Unlike prior work that instruments or wraps application code, we synthesize packet-level monitors that enforce protocols directly in the data plane. We develop algorithms to generate network-level monitors from session types and extend them to handle packet loss and reordering. We implement NEST in P4 and evaluate it on applications including microservice and network-function models, showing that network-level monitors can enforce realistic non-trivial protocols.

1
Introduction

Session types are a well-established formalism for specifying and
verifying message-passing
programs
[
28
,
27
,
20
,
72
,
13
]
. Whereas
conventional type systems model the types of data used by each process
(i.e., integers, strings, objects, etc.), session types also model

how
processes interact by sending and receiving messages.
For example, a process might receive a string from
A
, send an
integer to
B
, and then receive a boolean from
C
.

Session types have been used to verify implementations of complex
multiparty protocols, ensuring that each node only sends and receives
well-typed messages and that the system does not fail unexpectedly.
Although they originated in process algebras, session types have been
incorporated into mainstream programming languages including Rust, Go,
Java, OCaml, and Scala
[
15
,
56
,
14
,
32
,
38
,
42
,
17
,
16
,
29
,
30
,
65
,
59
,
57
,
61
,
33
,
75
]
.

Network-level monitoring: opportunities and challenges.

A
monitor
observes a system at runtime and checks conformance to a specification. For a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, it observes sent and received messages; on a violation, it can raise an alert or drop the message.
Runtime monitors are useful when the programs running on certain nodes cannot be statically type-checked, or for providing defense in depth. Most prior work has focused on runtime monitoring at the application
level
[
11
,
12
,
9
,
54
,
18
,
55
]
. This
paper asks a different question:
can we synthesize
monitors that enforce session types at the network level?

We have two primary motivations.

First, deploying monitors deeper in the network stack
places them beyond end-host control.
This gives stronger assurance in mixed-trust settings: in a public cloud, provider-managed
monitors can enforce session types even when tenants do not trust one another.
This motivation is illustrated in
Figure

1
,
which shows a network based on the “BookInfo”
application described by Istio
[
34
]
. The network
includes four end hosts, each implementing a different microservice
(
Info
,
Review
,
Details
, and

Ratings
) connected to each other and to an external

Client
.
first have the
Client
query the
Info
end host
for information on a book.
Info
then queries
Review

and
Details
for information, where the former also itself
queries
Ratings
. Finally,
Info
replies to the

Client
with the information obtained.

Without network-level session monitoring, faulty
or malicious code running on one of the end hosts (e.g.,

Info
) may generate invalid packets that reach other end
hosts, consuming network resources and potentially crashing
applications when they receive unexpected messages.

SW1
SW2
SW3
SW4
SW5
SW6

Without
NEST
:

faulty and malicious packets reach deep into the network

:

etwork

T

Figure 1
:
A network of microservices based on the Istio “BookInfo”
application
[
34
]
, highlighting a flow of bad packets
generated by a faulty implementation of the
Info

microservice, and how
NEST
can stop them.

Second, network-level monitors can run efficiently on suitable hardware, such as programmable switches and NICs. For instance, the P4 language for programming network switches
[
10
]

is designed for high performance, with line-rate processing of packets
[
31
,
39
]
.

Notably, since session types describe application-level protocols, their monitoring
at the network level breaks the classical network layering. This is not uncommon in modern networks, where classical layering abstraction
are sometimes broken to implement various functionalities. Middleboxes such as
NAT boxes, load balancers, proxies, and content caches realise functionality
at the transport layer (i.e., TCP/UDP or Layer 4) or above, by manipulating
packets at the network layer (i.e., IP or Layer 3): see the APLOMB paper

[
68
]
for a more detailed explanation,
including a survey of cloud operators. Moreover, existing network-layer devices already enforce simple
application-level patterns (e.g., firewalls, NAT, DPI) – but these policies
are limited: for example, NAT lets internal hosts receive packets only after
outbound traffic. Compared to these approaches, session types can express richer protocols and
come equipped with formal guarantees.

However, realizing network-level session type monitors requires addressing several challenges:

•

Session types can model rich behaviors that go well beyond static policies and simple firewalls. Hence, monitor synthesis must be
automatic
.

•

Monitors for session types must also be
stateful
, to track the current protocol state and accept or
reject packets accordingly. While basic connection tracking exists in devices such as stateful firewalls and NAT boxes,
session types bring significant complexity.

•

Network-level monitors must handle reordered packets and retransmissions after
loss
, often with limited hardware buffering. By contrast, existing application-level session monitors assume reliable transport (e.g., TCP) and enough buffering to reorder packets.

Contributions and outline.

To address these challenges, we design and implement
NEST
, a tool for generating and deploying network-level session monitors.
NEST
takes as input a set of local session types (represented with a Scala 3 embedded DSL) and their associated roles, generates corresponding P4 monitor representations, and uploads them on P4-enabled network devices. We develop the formal foundations of
NEST
, and evaluate it on a set of representative multiparty protocols using Mininet
[
44
]
, a realistic software-defined networking emulation platform. By enforcing session types at the network perimeter,
NEST
monitors can discard protocol-violating packets early, saving network resources and preventing invalid traffic from reaching downstream hosts and devices.
More broadly, our approach realises an “off-by-default” network
[
3
]
, where only authorized packets can traverse the network.

The main contributions of this paper are as follows:

Section

3

introduces
NEST
. Given
a set of session types written in a Scala 3 embedded DSL,
NEST
synthesizes network-layer monitors based on P4.

NEST
also generates an API for
writing end-host programs whose communication patterns are tracked
by our monitors (
Section

3.4
).

Section

4

presents the formal model at the basis of
NEST
monitors.
We introduce a novel monitor synthesis technique (
Definition

10
) tailored
for network-level session monitors that reject bad packets, while ensuring
soundness – i.e., messages from well-behaved end hosts
are not rejected (
Theorem

18
).

Section

5

presents a qualitative and quantitative
evaluation of
NEST
, showing that the monitors it generates
accept valid packets and reject invalid ones across a variety of
representative multiparty protocols.

Finally,
Section

6
discusses related work, and
Section

7
concludes with future directions.

NEST
is available in the companion artifact of this work
[
46
]
with instructions for reproducing the evaluation in
Section

5
.

]
from

and

. The

immediately with data (e.g., author and ISBN).

,

) and half-duplex. If needed, such local session types can be

and implemented in tools like Scribble,
\nu

).

By
projecting
a global session type to one role, we obtain
a
local session type
. The local session type in
Figure

3
describes the communication protocol
enacted by role
Info
. Although this simple example is linear, session types can also express
branching
and
recursion
:
branching session types are choice points where a role may send or receive one among several different messages
and possibly continue the session in a different way (see
Example

7
later on), and recursive session types
allow for repeating part of a session (see
Example

13
).

Local session types are typically used for compile-time type checking. In practice, many components cannot be session-typed (e.g., unsupported languages/frameworks or inaccessible participants such as the external
Client
in BookInfo). Even then, local session types remain a precise and expressive protocol specification language, so we use them as the basis for network monitoring.

3
NEST
: overview and end-host monitor design

This section overviews
NEST
, our toolkit for synthesizing and deploying network-level monitors from session type specifications.

Figure

4
summarizes the workflow.

Our design for
NEST
relies on two key assumptions. First, we assume we
are giving a local session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
that captures the intended behavior of
each node. For now, an intuitive understanding of session types will suffice.
The formal definition will be given in
Section

4
. Second, we
assume that the behavior of the devices at the edge of the network can be
specified in P4, a domain-specific language for programming network
switches
[
10
]
. To understand
NEST
, a deep understanding of P4 will not
be necessary. For now, there are two things to know: (i) P4 provides match-action
tables (MATs), which can be populated with entries at runtime to control how
packets are processed; (ii) P4 provides mutable registers, with associated read
and write operations, which can be used to implement stateful packet processing.
We will use both of these features in our design for
NEST
.

A MAT is essentially a table
where each row expresses a rule for recognising and handling packets. Intuitively, a MAT has two columns called
match
and
action
. The “match” column determines what is matched
(e.g., packet IP address, port, or P4 state register) and the expected values. The
“action” column determines which action is applied to a matching packet (e.g., forward,
drop, etc.).
The MATs defined by
NEST
encode a state machine: in each row, the “match” column specifies
how to match a packet based on the current state of the switch, and on the message label,
sender and receiver carried by the packet; then, the “action” column
specifies whether to accept the packet (transitioning to another state) or reject it.

Given an application’s (local) session type,
NEST
generates the following:

•

An
API
for sending and receiving messages in
the format expected by our network monitors. We describe the
message format in
Section

3.1
and
the session API generation in

Section

3.4
.

•

A set of
P4 routing table entries
(i.e., MAT entries) for
enforcing the session type on incoming traffic; these encode the
session type’s state progression as messages are sent and received by
participants.

Figure 4
:
Overview of
NEST
: the programmer writes a session type specification (left);

NEST
generates a Python session API, which end-host programs use for network communication (right).

NEST
also generates P4 switch configurations, which drop non-conformant packets.

Our monitors reject illegal packets at the network perimeter.
For example, in
Figure

1
, traffic from the faulty node is dropped at switch
SW2
.

Given a session type such as
Figure

6
and a role,
NEST
synthesizes a monitor in four steps
that effectively “bring down” an application-level session type specification
into the lower-level network layer:

1.

NEST
’s synthesis module constructs a state machine for the session-type monitor.

2.

NEST
converts this state machine into MAT entries, mapping each transition to one entry. For
Figure

6
, the generated entries are shown in
Figure

6
.

3.

NEST
then translates these MAT entries into entries for the P4 table
monitor_table
(
Section

3.1
), encoding sender/receiver roles and message labels as enumerated IDs.
At this stage,
NEST
can also generate monitoring logic for packet loss, duplication, and reordering in TCP connections (
Section

3.3
).

4.

Finally,
NEST
deploys the generated entries on P4-enabled devices acting as monitors.
2
2

2

The deployment phase uses the P4R-Type library
[
45
]

to statically ensure that the deployed entries conform to the
monitor_table

table definition.

Figure 6
:
A match-action table (MAT) generated by
NEST
from the session type in
Figure

. The message labels are shortened for brevity. In the “action” column,
accept
(m

) indicates that the monitor transitions to state m
X
upon accepting the message.

After deployment, the P4 device enforces session-type monitoring: upon
receiving a packet,
monitor_table
inspects the packet header and
accepts or rejects it.
NEST
also generates a session API for sending and
receiving packets with the headers expected by the monitors (
Section

3.4
).

Challenges.

The rest of this section addresses three practical challenges.

•

Accept/reject decisions and session state tracking (
Section

3.1
):

how should a P4 device correctly accept/reject packets while tracking multiple session types concurrently?

•

Shared entry points (
Section

3.2
):
how should one monitor handle multiple end hosts sharing an ingress point?

•

Packet loss, duplication, and reordering (
Section

3.3
):

how should
NEST
support transport protocols (e.g., TCP) that affect packet sequencing?

Assumptions and limitations.

We assume the devices at the edge of the network can be programmed in P4, so that all communication between protocol roles passes through monitored devices. The current version of
NEST
also assumes each session message fits into a single packet; ideas for lifting this restriction to handle fragmentation and additional transport protocols are discussed in
Section

7
.

3.1
Accepting/Rejecting Packets and Tracking Session-Type State

NEST
-generated monitors decide whether to accept or reject a packet by processing a dedicated

session header
(
Figure

8
). Each monitored-session packet must carry this header; others are rejected by default.
The P4 monitoring logic is implemented by the table
monitor_table

(
Figure

8
), which extracts from the session header the

message size
(required for deparsing the packet),

sender role
,
receiver role
, and

message label ID
, to decide whether to accept or reject
the packet.
3
3

3

The session header currently used by
NEST
(depicted in

Figure

8
) supports protocols with up to 15 distinct roles
and up to 63 distinct message labels. This bound can be increased if
needed, although doing so may be constrained by packet size and the memory
available on the P4 switch.

monitor_table
matches extracted session-header fields
against entries generated by
NEST
from a session-type MAT such as
Figure

6
. Packets matching at least one entry are accepted; otherwise, the default
reject
action drops them (line 13 in
Figure

8
).

monitor_table
’s
accept
action also inspects the

session ID
, allowing one device to distinguish and track many sessions in parallel.
When a packet with session ID
s
is accepted,
accept
uses
s
to index a P4 stateful register and update that session’s state.

The session header also carries a
session sequence number
, used to handle retransmissions (discussed in
Section

3.3
).

Computing the session monitor MAT.

NEST
’s monitoring strategy hinges on correctly synthesizing
the session-type monitor MAT that determines which packets are accepted or rejected. For instance, the state machine in
Figure

9(a)
corresponds to the
standard semantics of the session type in
Figure

6
– whereas
the state machine in
Figure

9(b)
is synthesized by
NEST
from the same session type, and then converted into the MAT in

Figure

6
. Observe that state m2 of the MAT allows

Info
to either send
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d\_req}}
to
Details
or
receive
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{r\_rsp}}
from
Review
– whereas these actions are
sequential in
Figures

6
and
9(a)
(first send, then receive). The difference between
Figure

9(a)
and

Figure

9(b)
is a consequence of “bringing down”
a session type specification to the network layer for monitoring purposes. This is because, while enforcing a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
,

NEST
’s network-level monitor may see packets that diverge from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
’s expected order.
The monitor must distinguish “bad” packets that violate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
from “good” packets delivered in a different order. We formalize this in
Section

4
and prove correctness.

3.2
Shared Entry Points

Although
Figure

1
shows one switch per end host,
NEST
also supports multiple end hosts per switch, even with different roles.
This raises a practical challenge:
how can one device monitor multiple roles concurrently?

Suppose a device must monitor roles
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
in session types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
be the monitors synthesized when these roles are monitored separately.
We build one monitor from these two using standard process-calculus techniques:

1.

Compute the labelled transition system (LTS) of the
parallel composition

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\parallel}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
in the style of CCS
[
51
]
, allowing synchronization when the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
accepts an outgoing message to
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
(or
vice versa
).

2.

Prune non-synchronising transitions where the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
accepts a message to/from
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
(or
vice versa
); in CCS terms, apply restriction
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}(\nu\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},\hskip-1.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}})\left({\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\parallel}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\right)}
.

3.

Deploy on the P4 device a monitor state machine matching this restricted composition, so the device monitors
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
together.
4
4

4

With a minor extension of the formal model introduced later
in
Section

4
, the behavior of a network using this joint
monitor for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
can be proven bisimilar to a network where
the end hosts of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
are connected to different devices,
matching the premises of our monitor correctness result
(
Theorem

18
).

3.3
Handling Packet Loss, Duplication, and Reordering

So far,
NEST
-generated monitor state machines and MATs
(
Figures

9(b)
and
6
)
cover the “core logic” from
Section

3.1
: for a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, they distinguish violating packets from out-of-order but valid packets.
This is also the focus of the formal synthesis in
Section

4
.
The result is a P4 monitor for reliable networks.

However, such monitors would often behave incorrectly in real-world
networks, where packets may experience loss, duplication, and reordering (even between packets with the same sender).
To address these issues, transport protocols such as TCP add transmission logic
to packets and may adjust their sending order to guarantee reliable
delivery.
Therefore,
NEST
is designed to be flexible with respect to transport
protocols between end hosts. In particular,
NEST
supports generation
of
TCP-oriented P4 monitors for session types
by augmenting a “core”
monitor state machine and MAT (like the ones in

Figures

9(b)
and
6
, which follow

Definition

10
) with transitions and checks for TCP socket setup
and teardown, packet acknowledgements, and retransmissions within a TCP
connection.
For example,
Figure

10
depicts the
TCP-oriented monitor obtained from the state machine in
Figure

9(b)

by adding TCP-specific transitions (dashed arrows) and checks.

•

The TCP-oriented monitor always accepts packets related to TCP
connection setup and teardown (i.e., TCP headers with the
SYN
or

FIN
flag set) without changing its state. It also accepts pure TCP acknowledgements (i.e., TCP headers with
ACK
) unless the
ACK
is piggybacked with a session header, in which case normal monitoring applies.

•

As mentioned in
Section

3.1
, the session header
carries a
session sequence number (SSN)
field, which records the total number
of messages sent by the sender so far. These sequence numbers are
tracked by our TCP-oriented monitors. The TCP-oriented monitor uses a detailed decision procedure based on SSNs; the details of this
decision procedure are simplified away for the transitions shown in
Figure

10
,
but are explained in
Figure

11
(essentially, the end states in
Figure

11

correspond to transitions that each state in
Figure

10
can take).
We explain the decision procedure:

1.

If an incoming packet’s SSN is less than or equal to the monitor’s stored SSN for that sender, the packet is accepted as a retransmission and does not update monitor state.

2.

Otherwise (SSN greater), the packet is first matched on sender and receiver
only
(ignoring label).
5
5

5

To match packets only on sender and receiver, the TCP-oriented monitor uses an additional table,

receiver_table
, a partial version of
monitor_table
that omits
hdr.session.label
.

This is done to distinguish truly invalid packets
from valid packets that arrive out of order. If the monitor was to match simultaneously on sender, recipient
and label, a failed match would be ambigious. The monitor must distinguish these two cases: in the first
case it must drop the session (see step 4), while in the second case it must just reject the packet (that will be
retransmitted).

3.

If both sender and recipient match, the monitor then matches the packet label.
If that matches, there are two cases. Suppose that the monitor is guarding the network border for end host
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
:

–

For each outgoing packet sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, if the packet SSN matches the next expected value (i.e., the stored SSN plus one), the monitor accepts the packet and updates its state. Instead, if the SSN is too high, the monitor drops the packet: this is because the SSN being too high means that some packets were lost, hence the monitor awaits their retransmission with the correct SSN.

–

For each incoming packet towards
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, the monitor accepts the SSN and updates its state without further checks: the monitor assumes that the SSN was already checked and accepted by the border monitor on the sender side.

4.

If recipient matches but label does not, the packet is rejected.
Moreover, if the monitor is guarding end host
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and the packet is sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with an SSN that is exactly one higher than stored SSN, the monitor rejects the packet
and
permanently closes the session -- because this indicates a session violation (not mere packet retransmission or reordering).
6
6

6

Closing the session, rather than only rejecting the packet, ensures the bad message is not later accepted as a retransmission after SSN advances.

m0
fail
m1
m2
Accept
(
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?\mathtt{req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}\,\checkmark}
)

Accept retransmit

or
Reject

Reject and

close

Accept
(
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}!\mathtt{r\_req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}\,\checkmark}
)

Accept retransmit

or
Reject

Reject and

close

Accept retransmit

or
Reject

Reject and

close

Figure 10
:
Initial states and transitions of the TCP-oriented
monitor generated from
Figure

9(b)
.

Figure 11
:
A flowchart showing how the TCP-oriented monitor processes incoming packets.

With this approach, our TCP-enabled monitors can handle packet loss (since TCP
eventually retransmits lost and unacknowledged packets), duplication (treated as
a special case of retransmission), and reordering of packets from the same
sender (by simply ignoring out-of-order packets and waiting for their
retransmission).

Limitations.

Our approach to monitoring TCP connections has limitations:

•

It requires TCP-oriented monitors to let all
ACK
packets pass
through the network. A malicious end host might abuse this to flood the
network with spoofed ACK messages, which the monitors would not reject. This is a common risk in networks with TCP services, and it may require
mitigations such as rate limiting to defend against TCP-based DoS
(denial-of-service) attacks.

•

Since the TCP-oriented monitors do not reject packets with low session
sequence numbers, a malicious or faulty sender could in principle send
messages with low session sequence numbers through the network without having
them rejected at the border.
To mitigate this, monitors could rate-limit forwarding of such packets, since they can be treated as retransmissions. The rate-limiting design is orthogonal to our monitoring logic, so we leave it to future work. Packets with lower-than-expected session sequence numbers are ignored by recipient-side programs using the
NEST
-generated APIs (
Section

3.4
).

•

The current version of
NEST
does not support packet fragmentation; we discuss remedies in
Section

7
.

3.4
API Generation for Session-Monitored Applications

Because our monitors require packets to carry the session header described in

Sections

3.1
and
3.3
,

NEST
generates an API for writing monitor-compliant applications.
The API hides session-header details and exposes human-readable message-label constants derived from the input session type, rather than numeric label IDs.
Our current prototype targets Python and supports the send/receive style in
Figure

3
.
By design, the API generated by
NEST
is minimalistic and does not
enforce the ordering of send/receive operations specified in the input session type.
We take advantage of this in our
evaluation (
Section

5.2
) to write programs that do
not
follow a
session type and show that
NEST
monitors correctly reject their messages.

The generated API is based on a
SessionManager
class that instantiates

Session
objects, each representing one session.
Each
Session
is created with a protocol and a session ID.
The API also maintains a queue of incoming messages, from which

Session
objects dequeue via
recvMsg()
. This prevents the host
program from incorrectly dropping messages (e.g., when a message is delivered
earlier than expected).

Automatic Session ID Propagation.

Beyond basic support for sends and receives, the
SessionManager
also handles automatic

propagation
of session IDs.
A program can create a
Session
without an ID; it then adopts the ID from the first
incoming message that carries an ID not already in use on that
SessionManager
.
Subsequent messages sent by that
Session
propagate that ID, and the
Session

dequeues only messages with that ID.

To support automatic ID propagation, the protocol must include an
initiator

that creates a new session ID and sends the first message(s) to one or more participants, who then
learn and propagate that ID.
All of the multiparty protocols we use in our evaluation (
Section

5
)
follow this pattern.

4
Proving the Correctness of
NEST
Monitors

In this section, we establish the correctness of
NEST
’s monitor synthesis,
i.e., the monitor state machine and MAT outlined at the end of
Section

3.1
. We focus on two key challenges for defining the “core logic” of our network-layer monitors:

1.

Monitors must correctly accept or reject
packets
immediately
, without resorting to buffering – which is assumed in previous work on session monitoring
[
8
,
9
,
18
]
but is often infeasible in network devices due to memory limitations.

2.

Monitors must make correct decisions even
when packets from different senders are delivered in an order
that does not match the expectations of the end hosts.

To isolate these challenges, we study monitor synthesis and
correctness in an idealised network where messages are delivered
instantaneously, without fragmentation, loss, duplication, or same-sender reordering; as explained in
Section

3.3
,
NEST
handles
these aspects
7
7

7

Except packet fragmentation, which is less common in modern networks configured
with a consistent maximum transmission unit (MTU). Support for fragmentation
is future work discussed in
Section

7
.
by augmenting the “core logic” of the monitors with additional checks and
transitions tailored to TCP as a transport protocol.
Challenges
1
and
2
, instead,
fundamentally affect monitoring logic, independently of the transport
protocol in use.

In
Section

4.1
we formalize networks where end-host behaviors are modeled as session types and protocol-violating packets may still reach hosts. In
Section

4.2
, monitors block those packets.

Section

4.3
then formalizes our monitor synthesis (
Definition

10
).
Finally,
Section

4.4
proves soundness: synthesized monitors do not reject traffic when all end hosts follow the enforced protocol (
Theorem

18
).

4.1
Session Types and Networks

In
Definitions

1
and
3
we model a network end host as a
(local) session type with a
multi-input queue
that stores incoming
messages from multiple senders, while preserving the order of messages from each
sender
[
19
]
. The idea is that the session type
models the behavior of a message-passing program, while the queue models the
end host’s ability to buffer incoming messages (e.g. in its network stack). This
modeling is standard in the session-types literature, except that
multi-output queues are often used instead of input queues.

Definition 1

(End host model)
.

The syntax of
session types with multi-input queues
is:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip 2mm}c@{\hskip 2mm}l@{\hskip 2mm}l}\textstyle\mbox{{Payload type, a.k.a.~sort}}\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Bool}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\cdots\\[2.84526pt]
\mbox{{Role}}\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Info}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Client}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Review}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{Details}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\cdots\\[2.84526pt]
\mbox{{Session type}}\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\sum}_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}{\oplus}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{{{{\&}}}}_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\\[2.84526pt]
\mbox{Input queue type}\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}\\[2.84526pt]
\mbox{Session type with input queue}\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

where
I\!\neq\!\emptyset
and the
message labels

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}
are pairwise distinct. We require session types to be closed and recursion variables to be guarded.

\lozenge

The type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\sum}_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}{\oplus}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}

represents an
internal choice
where the end host selects one
recipient role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}
and sends a message with label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}
carrying payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}
;
then, the interaction continues as specified in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
.
Dually,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{{{{\&}}}}_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}

represents an
external choice
where the end host awaits a message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}
with payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}
from sender
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
;
then, the interaction continues as specified in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
. The lists of possible roles
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
and payload types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
are provided as examples and can be extended as needed. The type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}
represents terminated
sessions, while
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}
represent recursion. We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}
as the
pairing of a session type with a multi-input queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
, which may contain
elements of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
, representing a
message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
sent by role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
.

Example 2
.

The formal definition of the session type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}
for the role
Info
from

Figure

3
is the following (for brevity, we shorten role names and message labels):

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{r\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{d\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}

\blacklozenge

Definition 3
.

The labeled transition system (LTS) semantics of session types (without queues) is defined as follows,
using the labels

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stIntSum{i \in I}{\roleP[i]}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}
{\stOutTag{\roleP[k]}{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]}}
{\stT[k]}}\ \textsc{S-IntC}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stExtSum{i \in I}{\roleP}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}
{\stInTag{\roleP}{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]}}
{\stT[k]}}\ \textsc{S-ExtC}\\
\\[-5.0pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\subst{\stT}{\stRecVar}{\stRec{\stRecVar}{\stT}}}{\stTagNoQueue}{\stTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stRec{\stRecVar}{\stT}}{\stTagNoQueue}{\stTi}}\ \textsc{S-Rec}\end{array}

The LTS semantics of session types with input queues is defined as follows:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stT}{\stOutTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}{\stTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stqPair{\stT}{\stQ}}
{\stSendTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}
{\stqPair{\stTi}{\stQ}}}\ \textsc{SQ-Send}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stT}{\stInTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}{\stTi}
\quad\stQ\equiv\stQCons{\stQMsg{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}{\stQi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stqPair{\stT}{\stQ}}
{\stIntTag{\dequeueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\stqPair{\stTi}{\stQi}}}\ \textsc{SQ-Deq}\\
\\[-10.0pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\stqPair{\stT}{\stQ}}
{\stEnqueueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}
{\stqPair{\stT}{\stQCons{\stQ}{\stQMsg{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}}}\ \textsc{SQ-Recv}\end{array}

where
\tau
denotes an internal transition that does not synchronize with others; in
SQ-Deq
,
\equiv
is the smallest congruence s.t.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}}})}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}
.

We use the symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
to denote either a label

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
(send message, emitted by rule
SQ-Send
) or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
(enqueue message, emitted by rule

SQ-Recv
).

\lozenge

In
Definition

3
, a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
transitions
by emitting labels representing an internal choice

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
or an external choice

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
, by rules

S-IntC
,
S-ExtC
, and
S-Rec
. For instance, for the session type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}
in
Example

2
, these rules yield the
transition system in
Figure

9(a)
.

When
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
is composed with a multi-input queue, rule
SQ-Send
says that internal choices of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
enable a “send”
action
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
. Rule
SQ-Deq
uses a standard queue congruence
\equiv
allowing for
swapping two queued messages with different senders: this enables the selective
dequeuing and consumption of the oldest queued message from each sender.
Then, rule
SQ-Deq

says that the session type can consume the oldest message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
(from the queue head, via congruence
\equiv
)
with an internal action (a.k.a. “
\tau
-action”)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}})}
;
this can happen only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
is an external choice that awaits a message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
,
and the oldest queued message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
satisfies these conditions. Finally, rule
SQ-Recv
allows an arbitrary message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}

to be received from the “outside world” and appended to the queue,
via a “receive” action
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
.

Example 4

(Semantics of session types with input queues)
.

Consider the session type from
Example

2
, paired with
an initially empty input queue:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{r\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{d\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\end{array}

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}
sends the expected message with label

\mathtt{req}
to the end host modeled by this session type. First,
the message is moved to the input queue by rule
SQ-Recv
, via a transition
{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?\mathtt{req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}}{}
,
resulting in the following session type with queue:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{r\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{d\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{req}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\end{array}

Since the head of the input queue now contains a message that matches one of the cases
in the topmost external choice, rule
SQ-Deq
enables a transition
{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\mathtt{req}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}})}}}{}

which consumes the queued message, resulting in:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{r\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{d\_req}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\end{array}

We can then immediately fire the internal choices by applying
SQ-Send

twice, with transitions
{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}!\mathtt{r\_req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}}{}
and
{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}!\mathtt{d\_req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}}{}
,
resulting in:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\end{array}

Now, suppose role
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
sends the expected message with label

\mathtt{r\_rsp}
first. We can enqueue the message with rule
SQ-Recv

and transition

{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}?\mathtt{d\_rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}}{}
,
leading to the following configuration. Note that the message from

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
is at the head of the queue, but cannot be consumed yet
because it does not match any of the cases in the topmost external choice:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{d\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\end{array}

Once we receive the response message from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
and enqueue it
with
SQ-Recv
, we can consume both messages by applying
SQ-Deq

twice. The first application of
SQ-Deq
below uses queue congruence

\equiv
(
Definition

3
) to swap the two messages in the
queue (since they have different senders) and bring the one from

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
to the head of the queue, enabling its consumption.

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{d\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\\
\phantom{\quad\text{({SQ-Recv})}}{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}?\mathtt{r\_rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}}{}\quad\text{({SQ-Recv})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{d\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{r\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}}\\
\phantom{\quad\text{({SQ-Deq}, {SQ-Deq})}}{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\mathtt{r\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}})}}}{}{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\mathtt{d\_rsp}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}})}}}{}\quad\text{({SQ-Deq}, {SQ-Deq})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\end{array}

Finally, we send the response to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}
and end the protocol:

\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}!\mathtt{rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}})}}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{({SQ-Send})}

\blacklozenge

Example 5

(Stuck session types and queues due to bad messages)
.

Rule
SQ-Recv
in

Definition

3
allows enqueuing a message that
the session type can never consume: this models the case
where an unexpected message is delivered to the end host from the
surrounding network. E.g., consider this configuration from
Example

4
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}\end{array}

If the surrounding network now delivers a message with label

\mathtt{unexpected}
from role
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
, we can enqueue it using

SQ-Recv
, with transition

{}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}?\mathtt{unexpected}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}}{}

leading to:

\begin{array}[]{c}\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{r\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\mathtt{d\_rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}\mathtt{rsp}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}})}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{\mathtt{unexpected}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\end{array}

This session type with queue is now stuck: it can only proceed by dequeuing a message
with label
\mathtt{r\_rsp}
from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
, but the oldest queued message from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
has label

\mathtt{unexpected}
. If more messages are queued, they will not be consumed either.

\blacklozenge

We model networks in
Definition

6
as parallel compositions of
roles with an end host behavior represented as a session type with an input queue.

Definition 6

(Network)
.

We define a network as:

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}\;\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}\qquad\text{where ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ does not occur in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}$}

with the following LTS semantics, using the labels

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\delta}\;\Coloneqq\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
from
Definition

3
: (for brevity we omit the symmetric rules of
Net-Par
and
Net-Comm
):

\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stqT}{\stExtTag}{\stqTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqT}}
{\netIOLab{\roleP}{\stExtTag}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqTi}}
}\ \textsc{Net-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}$}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stqT}{\stIntTag{\dequeueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\stqTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqT}}
{\netDequeueLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqTi}}
}\ \textsc{Net-Deq}\\
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\transition{\netN[1]}{\netLab}{\netNi[1]}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netN[1] \netPar\netN[2]\;}{\netLab}{\;\netNi[1] \netPar\netN[2]}}\ \textsc{Net-Par}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\transition{\netN[1]}{\netIOLab{\roleP}{\stSendTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\netNi[1]}
\quad\transition{\netN[2]}{\netIOLab{\roleQ}{\stEnqueueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\netNi[2]}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netN[1] \netPar\netN[2]\;}{\netCommLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}{\;\netNi[1] \netPar\netNi[2]}}\ \textsc{Net-Comm}\end{array}

\lozenge

In
Definition

6
above, rules
Net-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
and

Net-Deq
decorate a transition of a session type with input queue

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
by including the role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
that emitted the transition; specifically,
Net-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
is used when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}

emits or enqueues a message (via rules
SQ-Send
or
SQ-Recv
in
Definition

3
),
while
Net-Deq
is used when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
internally consumes a message from its queue
(via rule
SQ-Deq
in
Definition

3
). Rule
Net-Comm
says that if in the sub-network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{1}}
there is
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

sending a message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and in the sub-network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{2}}
there is
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}

that can receive that message, then the network advances with a communication
transition
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}
. The rightmost premise of rule
Net-Comm
implies that the message
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
is added to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s input queue, by
Net-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
and

SQ-Recv
in
Definition

3
. Also, by the same rules,
any

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{2}}
that includes
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can always receive

any
message from
any

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, i.e., rule

Net-Comm
allows invalid messages to be sent/received between end
hosts; see
Example

7
.

Example 7

(Bad messages in an unmonitored network)
.

Consider the following network with roles
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
: (for brevity, we omit the payload types and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}
s)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\;\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\;\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\hskip 20.44434pt\text{where}\hskip 10.22217pt\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sum}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}\end{array}\right.;\hskip 9.44456pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}\end{array}\right.;\hskip 9.44456pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}\end{array}

Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
can send either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
Meanwhile,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
expects to receive either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
. Instead,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}
just sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

By
Definition

6
the network could reduce as follows,
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and enqueued by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\Rule{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleP]}{\stQEmpty}}}
{\netIOLab{\roleP}{\stSendTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stEnd}{\stQEmpty}}}
\qquad\transition{\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQEmpty}}}
{\netIOLab{\roleQ}{\stEnqueueTag{\roleP}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}}
{\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQMsg{\roleP}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}}}}
{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleP]}{\stQEmpty}} \netPar\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQEmpty}}\;}
{\netCommLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}
{\;\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stEnd}{\stQEmpty}} \netPar\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQMsg{\roleP}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}}}}
{Net-Comm}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleP]}{\stQEmpty}} \netPar\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQEmpty}} \netPar\netPair{\roleR}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleR]}{\stQEmpty}}}
{\netCommLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\stqPair{\stEnd}{\stQEmpty}} \netPar\netPair{\roleQ}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleQ]}{\stQMsg{\roleP}{\stLabFmt{\stLabFmt{a}}}{}}} \netPar\netPair{\roleR}{\stqPair{\stT[\roleR]}{\stQEmpty}}}}\ \textsc{Net-Par}\end{array}

Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can consume the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and
later enqueue and consume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
: in this case, the
network reaches a successful final state where every end host is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}

with an empty queue.
Similarly, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
first, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
afterwards, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can consume both messages (like the last transitions of
Example

4
) reaching success. Therefore, in both cases,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
is a “good message” for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
However, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
consumes it and enters the branch where it expects

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
—but
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}

instead:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}l}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\;}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}}}()})}}}{\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}&\text{\tiny(by {Net-Comm}, {Net-Par})}\\
{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}\;}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({})})}}}{\;\;\,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}&\text{\tiny(by {Net-Deq}, {Net-Par})}\\
{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}\;}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}}()})}}}{\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}&\text{\tiny(by {Net-Comm}, {Net-Par})}\end{array}

Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
cannot consume
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
’s message and gets stuck –
similarly to
Example

5
. Note that in this execution, unlike the cases above,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
is a “bad message” for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

\blacklozenge

4.2
Monitored Networks

To model the pairing of an end host with a monitor that intercepts all its communications, in
Definition

8
below we combine a session type with queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
and a
generic
monitor

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
. For now we only assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
has an LTS
semantics with labels of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}
/

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}
to signal that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
accepts
the corresponding send/receive action by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{✘}}}
/
