{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{✘}}}
to signal that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
rejects
them. (We present a concrete instantiation of
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
in
Definition

10

below.)

Definition 8
.

We define a
monitored session type (with input queue)
as:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip 2mm}c@{\hskip 2mm}l@{\hskip 2mm}l}\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\hskip 5.69054pt&\Coloneqq\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}}\hfil\hskip 5.69054pt&\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

where
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
is a monitor. We also define the monitored session type semantics:

\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stqT}
{\stExtTag}
{\stqTi}
\qquad\transition{\monM}
{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}
{\monMi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mstPair{\stqT}{\monM}}
{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}
{\mstPair{\stqTi}{\monMi}}}\ \textsc{M-Good}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stqT}
{\stOutTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}
{\stqTi}
\quad\transition{\monM}
{\monLabNo{\stOutTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\monMi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mstPair{\stqT}{\monM}}
{\monLabNo{\stOutTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\mstPair{\stqTi}{\monMi}}}\ \textsc{M-BadOut}\\
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\monM}
{\monLabNo{\stInTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\monMi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mstPair{\stqT}{\monM}}
{\monLabNo{\stInTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\mstPair{\stqT}{\monMi}}}\ \textsc{M-BadIn}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\stqT}{\stIntTag{\dequeueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\stqTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mstPair{\stqT}{\monM}}
{\monLabSilent{\dequeueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\mstPair{\stqTi}{\monM}}}\ \textsc{M-Dequeue}\end{array}

\lozenge

By rule
M-Good
in
Definition

8
, the pair

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}}
performs a transition
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}\,\checkmark}
if

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
explicitly accepts the send/receive action
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
emitted by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
. Rule
M-BadOut
says that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
can reject and drop a message sent by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
. Rule
M-BadIn
says that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
can reject and drop an incoming
message before it lands in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
’s input queue. By rule
M-Dequeue
,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
cannot interfere with the
internal action that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
performs when consuming a message from its input
queue. In
Definition

9
below we monitor networks
(
Definition

6
), by adding a monitor to each end host.

Definition 9

(Monitored Network)
.

We define a monitored network as:

{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}}\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime}}\;\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\;{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}}}\qquad\text{where ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ does not occur in ${\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}$}

with the following semantics, using the labels

{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\eta}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}}}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\checkmark}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{✘}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}

(for brevity we omit the symmetric rules of
MNet-Par
and
MNet-Comm
):

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\monT}{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}{\monTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mnetPair{\roleP}{\monT}}
{\mnetIOLabYes{\roleP}{\stExtTag}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\monTi}}
}\ \textsc{MNet-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}$-Good}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\monT}{\monLabNo{\stExtTag}}{\monTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mnetPair{\roleP}{\monT}}
{\mnetIOLabNo{\roleP}{\stExtTag}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\monTi}}
}\ \textsc{MNet-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}$-Bad}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\transition{\mnetN[1]}{\mnetLab}{\mnetNi[1]}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mnetN[1] \mnetPar\mnetN[2]\;}{\mnetLab}{\;\mnetNi[1] \mnetPar\mnetN[2]}}\ \textsc{MNet-Par}\\
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\monT}{\monLabSilent{\dequeueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\monTi}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\netPair{\roleP}{\monT}}
{\mnetDequeueLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}
{\netPair{\roleP}{\monTi}}
}\ \textsc{MNet-Deq}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\transition{\mnetN[1]}{\mnetIOLabYes{\roleP}{\stSendTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\mnetNi[1]}
\qquad\transition{\mnetN[2]}{\mnetIOLabYes{\roleQ}{\stEnqueueTag{\roleP}{\stLab}{\stS}}}{\mnetNi[2]}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\mnetN[1] \mnetPar\mnetN[2]\;}{\mnetCommLab{\roleP}{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}{\;\mnetNi[1] \mnetPar\mnetNi[2]}}\ \textsc{MNet-Comm}\end{array}

\lozenge

In
Definition

9
, each end host is modeled as a role

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with a session type (with an input queue) representing the end host
behavior, equipped with a monitor. Rules

MNet-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
-Good
and
MNet-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
-Bad
annotate
accepted and rejected actions (with labels
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}\,\checkmark}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{✘}}}
from
Definition

8
) with the end host
role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
where such actions occurred.
The rule
MNet-Comm
is different from
Net-Comm

in
Definition

6
, because it only allows communications between
two end hosts if their respective monitors
accept their outgoing and incoming message; this is because the transitions in the premises of rule

MNet-Comm
(denoting the acceptance of a message send and
enqueuing) can only be fired via rule
MNet-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
-Good
.

4.3
Synthesizing Network Layer Monitors from Session Types

We now provide a concrete instantiation of
monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
from
Definition

8
: in
Definition

10
we formalize how to synthesize a
network-layer
monitor state
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
from a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
. In
Section

3.1
(
Figure

9
) we
anticipated that the monitor state machine
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
differs from
that of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
. This is because

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
has to meet several non-trivial requirements:

(R1)

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
must accept all messages that an end
host implementing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
may send/receive to/from the network, depending on

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
’s state;

(R2)

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
should reject invalid messages that an end host
implementing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
should
not
send/receive, again depending on

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
’s state;

(R3)

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
may receive messages from different senders in an order that
does not match
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
’s expectations (due to the network semantics),
8
8

8

In
Section

3.3
we also addressed the
issue of out-of-order delivery of messages
from the same sender
,
which is orthogonal and handled by protocols like TCP.
and yet,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
must accept the valid (“good”) messages while still
rejecting the invalid (“bad”) ones;

(R4)

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
must decide whether to accept or reject a message

immediately
,
without buffering
, to accommodate the limited
memory and processing power of most network devices.

Definition 10

(Session-type-based network monitor)
.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
to represent the state of a monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M}
based on a
session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, with semantics given by the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stIntSum{i \in I}{\roleP[i]}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}}
{\monLabYes{\stSendTag{\roleP[k]}{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]}}}
{\enc{\stT[k]}}}\ \textsc{STMon-IntC}\hskip 10.22217pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stExtSum{i \in I}{\roleP}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleP}{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]}}}
{\enc{\stT[k]}}}\ \textsc{STMon-ExtC}}\\[28.45274pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\exists\text{ maximal } \emptyset\neq K \subseteq I,\roleQ,\stLab,\stS:\quad\forall i \in I: \roleQ\neq\roleP[i]\quad\forall k \in K: \exists\stTi[k]:{\transition{\enc{\stT[k]}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\enc{\stTi[k]}}}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stIntSum{i \in I}{\roleP[i]}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\enc{\stIntSum{k \in K}{\roleP[k]}{\stChoice{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]} \stSeq\stTi[k]}}}}\ \textsc{STMon-IntPfx}\\[28.45274pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\exists\text{ maximal } \emptyset\neq K \subseteq I,\roleQ,\stLab,\stS:\quad\roleQ\neq\roleP\quad\forall k \in K: \exists\stTi[k]:{\transition{\enc{\stT[k]}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\enc{\stTi[k]}}}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stExtSum{i \in I}{\roleP}{\stChoice{\stLab[i]}{\stS[i]} \stSeq\stT[i]}}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLab}{\stS}}}
{\enc{\stExtSum{k \in K}{\roleP}{\stChoice{\stLab[k]}{\stS[k]} \stSeq\stTi[k]}}}}\ \textsc{STMon-ExtPfx}\\[28.45274pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\transition{\enc{\subst{\stT}{\stRecVar}{\stRec{\stRecVar}{\stT}}}}{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}{\enc{\stTi}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stRec{\stRecVar}{\stT}}}{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}{\enc{\stTi}}}\ \textsc{STMon-Rec}\hskip 20.44434pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\noTransition{\enc{\stT}}{\monLabYes{\stExtTag}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stT}}{\monLabNo{\stExtTag}}{\enc{\stT}}}\ \textsc{STMon-Bad}\end{array}\small

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
to represent the monitor state defined
as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}

where
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}\mathbin{\mathbf{\triangleright}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{1}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}})}}\mathbin{\cdot}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\,\cdots\,}\mathbin{\cdot}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{n}}}\mathbin{\mathbf{\triangleright}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{n}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}})}}}}})\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{1}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{1}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{1}}})}\,\checkmark}}}}{{\cdots}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{n}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{n}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{n}}})}\,\checkmark}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}
.

\lozenge

By rule
STMon-IntC
in
Definition

10
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
is an
internal choice, then
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
accepts the corresponding send actions and
updates its state. Dually, by rule
STMon-ExtC
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
is an
external choice, then
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
accepts the corresponding receive actions and
updates its state. This reflects requirements
(R1)
and
(R4)
.

Rules
STMon-IntPfx
and
STMon-ExtPfx
allow
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
to
accept an incoming message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
with payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
from role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
even if the shape of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
does not expect a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
right now. This is necessary to satisfy requirements
(R3)
and
(R4)
.
By the premises of these rules, acceptance is allowed only if role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
is
not
an immediate recipient/sender in the internal/external choice
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, and at least one monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{k}}}}\right\rrbracket}
(where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{k}}
is a continuation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
)
can indeed accept that message by firing a transition
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{k}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}_{k}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.
If these conditions hold, then
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
performs the same accepting transition and becomes
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}
has the same shape as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, except:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}
keeps only the branches of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
(indexed by the maximal set

K\subseteq I
) that could accept
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}

in their future transitions; and

2.

The continuation of each kept branch is reduced to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}_{k}}
(for
k\in K
).

In other words, after accepting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
,
rules
STMon-IntPfx
and
STMon-ExtPfx

“prune”
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
by removing all the branches that, if taken, could not
possibly accept
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
in their future transitions. Note that, these rules can fire only if
K\neq\emptyset
:
there must therefore be at least one branch of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
that can accept
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}

in its future transitions.

Rule
STMon-Rec
unfolds recursion. Rule
STMon-Bad
rejects
any send/receive action that
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
does not explicitly accept,
per requirements
(R2)
and
(R4)
.
Finally,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
represents the monitor state obtained by
feeding all messages in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
as inputs to
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
, which must
accept all of them: i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
is undefined if

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
does not accept some message in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
.

Examples.

To illustrate how our session monitors work, we present
three examples:

•

Example

11
shows how a monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
can
accept messages that arrive in an order different from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
’s expectations, and
how doing so restricts the inputs and outputs it will accept next.

•

Example

12
revisits

Example

4
to track how a monitor evolves
alongside the end host’s session type and input queue.

•

Example

13
shows that some session types yield
infinite-state monitors under
Definition

10
, which
cannot be represented using a finite number of states in P4
(
Section

3
).

•

A further monitor-reduction example appears in

Example

21
in the appendix.

Example 11
.

Consider the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
from
Example

7
: (for brevity, we omit the payload types)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\end{array}\right.

By
Definition

10
, the corresponding monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
can
immediately accept not only the two top-level messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, but also
the successive messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
– which appear later in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
, but may be
delivered earlier by the surrounding network. For the top-level inputs we have:

{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}({})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}\hskip 10.22217pt\text{and}\hskip 10.22217pt{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}\hskip 20.44434pt\text{\footnotesize(by {STMon-ExtC} in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:monitor-state-st})}

Notice that the message sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
restricts what the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}

accepts from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
afterwards. If
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
is deployed in the network of

Example

7
to monitor end host
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, then, if

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
, the monitor will accept
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
(which is a “good” message in this state); instead, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
, the monitor will reject
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}

(which is a “bad” message in this state).

Notably, the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
can also immediately accept the
messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
The transitions
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}}\,\checkmark}

and
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}}\,\checkmark}

are fired by the following derivations:

\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\Rule{}
{
{\transition{\enc{\roleR\stExtC\stLabFmt{a'} \stSeq\stEnd}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleR}{\stLabFmt{a'}}{}}}
{\enc{\stEnd}}}
}
{STMon-ExtC}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stT[\roleQ]}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleR}{\stLabFmt{a'}}{}}}
{\enc{\roleP\stExtC\stLabFmt{a} \stSeq\stEnd}}}\ \textsc{STMon-ExtPfx}\hskip 10.22217pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\Rule{}
{
{\transition{\enc{\roleR\stExtC\stLabFmt{b'} \stSeq\stEnd}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleR}{\stLabFmt{b'}}{}}}
{\enc{\stEnd}}}
}
{STMon-ExtC}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stT[\roleQ]}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleR}{\stLabFmt{b'}}{}}}
{\enc{\roleP\stExtC\stLabFmt{b} \stSeq\stEnd}}}\ \textsc{STMon-ExtPfx}

Observe that the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b^{\prime}}}
) from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
causes rule
STMon-ExtPfx
to “prune” the session type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
in the monitor state, only keeping the branch where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
(resp
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
) from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
can be received. Therefore, if
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
is deployed in the network of

Example

7
to monitor end host
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, it will accept

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
even before
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
: this is because in this state it is still possible for the
end host to consume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
without getting stuck. Then, after accepting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
:

1.

the monitor will accept
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
—which is a
“good” message in this state, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
can consume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
from
the end host’s input queue. However,

2.

the monitor will reject
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
—which is a
“bad” message in this state, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
cannot consume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
(as
shown at the end of
Example

7
).

This strategy for accepting messages is necessary because, depending on the
overall multiparty interaction, messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
may be delivered
before those from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. This phenomenon is further illustrated in

Example

12
below.

\blacklozenge

.

Consider again the session type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}

(with an input queue)
in

. We

and an empty input queue with a monitor
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}

, obtaining
{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
(by
Definition

8
);
we explain how their respective states change as they send and receive messages, according to
Definition

8
.

In
Figure

9
, the session type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}
and its monitor
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
begin in state s0
and m0 in respectively. At this point,

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
will
only
allow the message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{req}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}
from the
Client

(represented by
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}
) to go through. Once the message arrives,

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
will accept it by progressing to state m1
(by
STMon-ExtC
). As part of accepting the message, the monitor
forwards it to the end host input queue, and then the session type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keywords3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{Info}}}}}}}}}
consumes it (by
SQ-Recv
and
SQ-Deq
), reaching state s1. The session
type can then immediately progress to state s2, then s3, by sending

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}!\mathtt{r\_req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}!\mathtt{d\_req}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Int}}})}
to
Review
(role

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
) and
Details
(role
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
) respectively.
Let’s assume that the monitor
forwards both of these messages before it sees a response, progressing to state m2, then m4.

Now, the session type (now in state s3) expects a response from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
and then from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
,
but there is no guarantee that the responses will be delivered in this exact order. The monitor (now in state m2) accounts for this. Suppose that the monitor receives
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}}?\mathtt{d\_rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}
as the
first response. The monitor accepts the message, and progresses to state m6
while forwarding the message. The session type, however, does not progress
immediately, but remains in state s3 as the message from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
in its queue does not
match any of the branches in its external choice. (See the execution in
Example

4
.)

Eventually, the monitor also receives the response message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}?\mathtt{r\_rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}
from
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
, and
progresses to state m7 while forwarding the message to the session type’s
input queue—which can then finally dequeue both messages from

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{d}}}
, proceeding to state s4, then s5.

Finally, the session type (now in state s5) sends the message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}!\mathtt{rsp}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Str}}})}
to
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}
and progresses
to state s6; the monitor (now in state m7) accepts the outgoing message and progresses to
state m8, at which point the protocol has finished.

\blacklozenge

Example 13

(On unmonitorable session types)
.

Consider the session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}

By

and

. Moreover, the same monitor can

Consequently, the LTS of this session-type monitor has infinitely many states.

\blacklozenge

Our monitor synthesis implementation (
Section

3
) rejects session
types such as
Example

13
, because the monitor state machine is constrained by the (often limited) amount
of storage available in network hardware. To avoid infinite-state monitors, the session types being monitored cannot
receive unbounded inputs from multiple roles. Many communication protocols
involve “request-response” patterns that keep our monitors
finite-state, including all the examples we evaluate in
Section

5
.

4.4
Soundness of Session-Types-Based Network Monitoring

A non-negotiable feature of session-type-based monitors from

Definition

10
is
soundness
: this means that monitors must
not reject “good” messages—i.e., monitors must not produce false
positives and interfere with a well-behaved network. We formalize this intuition by considering a monitored network
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}}
where all
monitors
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
(for all roles
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}}
) are
based on session types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
that are mutually compatible, and
each end host for role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
behaves according to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
. In
Theorem

18
we show that the monitors in such
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}}

are
transparent
: they never disrupt communications between
well-behaved hosts.

We now develop the technical machinery for this result. In

Definition

14
we define a
consistent instrumentation

where each end host is given a monitor matching the end host specification.

Definition 14

(Consistent Instrumentation of a Network)
.

Given a network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, we define its
consistent monitor
instrumentation

{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
as:

{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\mathop{\parallel}\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}_{i}}}}}}}\right)}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\mathop{\parallel}\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}:{\left({\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}_{i}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}_{i}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\right)}}}}

\lozenge

For an arbitrary
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, the instrumented network
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
may reject
messages if the underlying session types are not “compatible” with each other,
e.g., some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
may send to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
a message that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
does not
expect. For instance, if
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is the network in
Example

7
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
would reject messages as shown in
Example

11
.

To prove monitor soundness, we must ensure that monitored session types are compatible: we require
output-liveness
as in
Definition

15

below. Our output-liveness is a weaker variant of the
typing context liveness
property
adopted in many session typing papers
[
66
,
6
,
22
,
60
,
74
]
:
like the standard liveness definition, we require that messages sent by a participant are eventually consumed by the intended recipient
(assuming fair scheduling)
– but unlike the standard definition, we do
not
require that a participant awaiting a message will eventually receive one. In other words, our
Definition

15
does
not
allow a network to have
orphan messages that are sent and queued but never consumed – but it allows a network to have participants
that wait forever to receive messages which are never sent.

Definition 15

(Output-Live Session Type Networks, adapted from
[
22
, Def. 4.7]
)
.

A
network path
is a possibly infinite sequence of pairs of network
configurations
({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{i}})_{i\in I}
, where
I=\{0,1,\ldots\}
is a set of consecutive natural numbers and,

\forall i\in I
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{i}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({\gamma_{i}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}_{i}}}
. We say that
a network path is fair
iff,
\forall i\in I
:

1.

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{i}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{}
​,
then
\exists k\in I
such that
k\geq i
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}^{\prime}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k+1}}}
;

2.

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{i}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{}
​,
then
\exists k\in I
such that
k\geq i
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k+1}}}
.

We say that
a network path is output-live
iff,
taking any
i\in I
and letting
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\mathop{\parallel}\limits_{j\in J}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{j}}}:{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{j}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{j}}}})}}}}
,

\forall j\in J
we have that
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{j}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}
,
then
\exists k\in I
such that
k\geq i
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{j}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N_{k+1}}}
.

We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is output-live
if every fair path beginning with

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is output-live.

\lozenge

In
Definition

15
, a “path” represents a possible network execution.
A path is
fair
if it eventually
allows enabled communications between participants (item
1
) and every enabled dequeuing action (item
2
); note that, in item
1
, the existence of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}

establishes that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
is ready to send some message
(with an internal choice) that a recipient
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
is ready to enqueue
– while
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}}
are the actual recipient and message
selected by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in this execution path. A path is
output-live
if every
queued message is eventually consumed by its intended recipient.

Example 16

(Output-live networks)
.

Consider the network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
in
Example

7
:
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is not output-live, because it has e.g. a fair path where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}

sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
to

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, hence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
cannot consume the queued message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a^{\prime}}}

from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
. In contrast, the network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
obtained by replacing the session type of

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}

is output-live, because in every fair path of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
every queued message
is eventually consumed. Also, all the examples we evaluate in
Section

5
are output-live.

\blacklozenge

To ensure monitoring correctness,
we require a further
half-duplex
assumption to control monitor state-space size. Intuitively, a network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is
half-duplex
if, for any two roles

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, data can flow only in one direction at a time, i.e., if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
sent a
message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
must consume that message before sending
another message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
(and
vice versa
). In other words,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can only communicate by “taking turns”
– thus, the input queue of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
can contain a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
only
if the input queue of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
does
not
contain any message from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. All the examples we evaluate in
Section

5
are half-duplex.
(For the formal definition of half-duplex and an example showing why we need it,
see
Definition

22
and
Example

23
in the appendix.)

To state our monitoring soundness result, we use
Definition

17
to
define when two networks have equivalent internal behavior, i.e., when they communicate and consume messages in the same way.

Definition 17

(Internal Bisimulation)
.

Let
\phi
be an annotation to distinguish
\tau
-labels of the form
\tau({\phi})
. We say that
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
is an internal bisimulation relation
iff,
whenever
(s,t)\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
,

1.

if
{s}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau({\phi})}}{s^{\prime}}
,
then
\exists t^{\prime}
such that
{t}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau({\phi})}}{t^{\prime}}

and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
;

2.

if
{t}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau({\phi})}}{t^{\prime}}
,
then
\exists s^{\prime}
such that
{s}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau({\phi})}}{s^{\prime}}

and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
.

We say
s
and
t
are
internally bisimilar
, written
s\mathrel{\overset{\tau}{\sim}}t
, iff
there is an internal bisimulation
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
such that
(s,t)\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
.

\lozenge

We now have all the ingredients to state and prove that our monitors are

sound
by runtime verification standards
[
4
]
, i.e., they have no false positives.
9
9

9

Another desirable property for monitors is

completeness
, i.e., having no false negatives. Here we focus on soundness because it is non-negotiable, and completeness may
not be achievable together with soundness: we discuss these issues in

Section

7
.
In our setting, this means they never misclassify a good message as bad
and never interfere with well-behaved end hosts, provided the implemented protocol is output-live and half-duplex.
(Proof in
Section

A.1
.)

Theorem 18

(Monitor Soundness)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is output-live and half-duplex, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}\mathrel{\overset{\tau}{\sim}}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
.

Remark 19

(On determining output-liveness and half-duplex properties)
.

Output-liveness (
Definition

15
) is generally undecidable, since two
session types with unbounded queues can encode a Turing
machine
[
5
, Theorem 2.5]
. It can,
however, be guaranteed by decidable approximations such as bounded model
checking or projection from a global type
[
43
,
47
]
. Similar techniques can be used to
ensure half-duplex execution (
Definition

22
,
[
70
]
). These checks are orthogonal to this work.
The protocols we evaluate in
Section

5
are output-live and half-duplex,
with bounded queue sizes,
so they have finite LTSs and are amenable to model checking.

Remark 20

(On the monitor rejection strategy)
.

The particular rejection strategy for session-type monitors does not affect
the soundness
Theorem

18
, which only concerns accepted
behavior. Concretely,
Definition

10
says that the monitor’s verdict is
not persistent: if a monitor rejects a message, then it can still accept a
subsequent valid message.
Theorem

18
would still hold, for example, if rule

STMon-Bad
in
Definition

10
always moved
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}

to
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}}\right\rrbracket}
after an invalid send/receive, making the rejection
verdict persistent and blocking any subsequent message to/from the end host. Indeed,
NEST
’s TCP-oriented monitors (described in

Section

3.3
) use persistent verdicts: they
block all end host communications (by dropping the whole TCP connection)
when the end host sends a “bad” message (as we show in

Section

5.2
).

5
Empirical Evaluation

In this section, we evaluate
NEST
’s correctness and effectiveness against
the following research questions:

(Q1)

Can
NEST
generate monitors for
non-trivial multiparty protocols?

(Q2)

Do
NEST
-generated
monitors accommodate correct communication without
interference, while rejecting incorrect messages even in the
presence of packet loss, duplication, and reordering (when
using the TCP-oriented monitors described in

Section

3.3
)?

(Q3)

Does
NEST
support monitoring
multiple concurrent sessions?

We first describe test cases and setup (
Section

5.1
), then analyze a representative case (
Section

5.2
), and finally report aggregate monitoring statistics (
Section

5.3
).
Our evaluation is based on simulated networks including nodes running
BMv2

[
58
]
, a P4-enabled software switch. We adopt this setting because careful hardware experiments would require significant engineering efforts that are orthogonal to the main contributions of this paper. The drawback of this choice is that we cannot perform meaningful performance evaluations, as BMv2 does not reflect the performance characteristic of real P4-enabled hardware. However, previous work suggests that, if the
NEST
-generated P4 monitors can be compiled to a hardware platform without exceeding the available resources, then they will run with little to no overhead, no matter how much traffic the device is processing, up to its limit. For example, Figures 9 and 10(c) of
[
39
]
show constant throughput and latency, even when the P4 switch is fully loaded.

5.1
Test Cases and Evaluation Setup

Methodology.

To address question
(Q1)
, we selected non-trivial test cases based on real-world multiparty protocols, with varying numbers of participants and branching/looping structures (
Table

1
).
We describe each test case in
Appendix

B
, together with
the local session type of each participant.
In each test case, participant behavior is specified as a
session type, and the full system is output-live and half-duplex (
Definitions

15
and
22
).
This guarantees the formal preconditions for sound monitoring
(
Theorem

18
) and provides the basis for empirically evaluating
question
(Q2)
– for which here we also consider
networks with packet loss, duplication, and reordering (that are are not
formally covered by
Theorem

18
).

:
The 10 test case protocols on which we evaluated
NEST
. For each one of them we report the number of participants, and whether
the session type representation includes branching and/or loops.

Evaluation Setup.

For each test case in
Table

1
, we set up a simulated
network in which end hosts (i.e., multiparty-protocol participants) communicate via P4-enabled border switches, as in

Figure

1
. We use Mininet
[
52
]
, which allows us to simulate networks with
different topologies, end hosts, and switch configurations. The simulated network includes nodes running
BMv2

[
58
]
, a P4-enabled software switch.
These switches perform regular forwarding when monitoring is disabled, and deploy/run our

NEST
-generated monitors to evaluate question
(Q1)
.

To evaluate question
(Q2)
, for each test case
in
Table

1
we provide correct and faulty
participant implementations and assess whether

NEST
-generated monitors accept or reject packets as expected.
Each variant is implemented in Python and executed on Mininet using the
NEST
-generated API (
Section

3.4
).

To stress-test whether question
(Q2)
can be answered positively under different transports, we implement
each scenario in
Table

1
with both UDP and
TCP communication. For TCP, we evaluate the
behavior of our TCP-oriented monitors
(
Section

3.3
) both on a perfectly
reliable network, and on an unreliable network with packet loss,
duplication, and delay: specifically, we configure the Mininet end hosts to drop 1% of all incoming
packets, duplicate 1% of all outgoing packets, and delay the sending of
outgoing packets by a variable amount (up to 100 ms).
The delay perturbs packet ordering both across senders (where arbitrary interleavings are allowed) and for packets from the same sender (where out-of-order packets are rejected and retransmitted).

To evaluate question
(Q3)
, we run multiple concurrent
sessions per test case (typically 10 to 50). All experiments were conducted on a machine with an 8-core, 3 GHz CPU and 32 GB of RAM,
running Ubuntu 22.04.

5.2
Assessing the Correctness of
NEST
-Generated Session Monitors

To illustrate our evaluation of question
(Q2)

(i.e., whether
NEST
-generated monitors accept/reject messages
correctly), we focus on one of the 10 test
cases in
Table

1
: the BookInfo
protocol
[
34
]
, our running example from

Section

1
. We applied the same assessment to every
test case in
Table

1
and observed similar
results, so the analysis below is representative. We also briefly report results for the VPN
test case, which is structurally richer than BookInfo and covers all
session-type features (branching and nested loops). Full
details for the other test cases appear in
Appendix

B
.

⬇

Figure 12
:
Faulty implementation of the session type in
Figure

6
.

For this representative case,
Figure

13
reports cumulative packets
received across all hosts under different configurations (UDP or TCP,
reliable or unreliable TCP networks, faulty or correct hosts, and with or
without runtime monitors).
The bars show the median packet counts
over five runs, with the maximum and minimum counts shown with the black dot/line
(most noticeable in
Figure

13(a)
).

((a))

Packets observed for the BookInfo example over different network configurations.

((b))

:
Incoming packets observed across all hosts, in different examples and configurations.

((a))

BookInfo, reliable network with TCP

((b))

((c))

((d))

VPN, unreliable network with TCP

Figure 14
:
Incoming packets observed across all hosts over time for BookInfo (top row) and VPN (bottom row).

The “correct traffic” bars in
Figure

13(a)

(solid red and blue columns) represent BookInfo runs where each end host correctly implements the session protocol.

NEST
-generated monitors do not reject any packets: the same number of
packets is observed in both monitored (blue) and unmonitored (red) networks. This is consistent with our soundness
Theorem

18
.

In BookInfo configurations with “faulty traffic”, the end host for
participant
Info
runs a program that does
not
conform
to the expected protocol and sends incorrect messages to other hosts. Specifically, it sends additional messages to

Review
and
Details
(
Figure

12
).
Key observations:

•

The total number of packets with faulty traffic on
unmonitored networks (red hatched columns) is higher than that for
correct traffic (red solid columns), because all faulty
packets reach their destination end host.

•

By contrast, there is no increase in packets observed under
monitoring (blue hatched columns), because each faulty packet
(which is UDP in this case) is dropped by the session monitor for

Info
and does not enter the network; hence, the faulty packet
is not observed by other end hosts.

•

With TCP transport and TCP-oriented monitors, incorrect packets cause a significant
drop in the observed packet counts: this is due to the session-closing mechanism in

Section

3.3
, which blocks a session as soon as a
faulty packet is observed. In
Figure

14

it is possible to see that the
NEST
TCP monitors keep the observed packet count
persistently low in faulty runs, as no further packets are observed after a faulty
one causes its session to be closed.

•

On unreliable networks with TCP,
there are slight differences for observed packet counts between monitored and
unmonitored examples. This is because (1) monitors may drop out-of-order packets,
which may slightly reduce packet count, or slightly increase it due to
retransmissions; and (2) we perform random packet drops, reordering, and
delays to simulate unreliable networks. Consequently, some out-of-order and
retransmitted packets may be dropped or received by the end host multiple
times, causing variations in packet counts. Crucially, the plots
show that our TCP monitors do not block correct traffic – otherwise they
would disrupt TCP connections and significantly drop the “monitored, correct
traffic” packet count like the “monitored, faulty traffic” TCP count.

The same trend appears for VPN in the bottom row of
Figure

14
.
There, faulty packets are sent later than in BookInfo, so monitored faulty runs observe more packets before session closure. Corresponding plots for the remaining examples appear in
Appendix

C
.

These observations show that our monitors do not disrupt
well-behaved programs and correctly reject non-conformant messages, for non-trivial cases: therefore,
questions
(Q1)
and
(Q2)
can be answered positively. Question
(Q3)
can be answered positively as well, as these observations hold when running multiple concurrent sessions for each test case.

Table 2
:
Statistics collected by our
NEST
-generated session monitors for the test
cases in
Table

1
. For each test case, we consider both
correct and faulty implementations, using either UDP or TCP as the transport
protocol, with TCP evaluated under both reliable and unreliable network
conditions. Column

reports the total number of packets

A

the total number of

re
