ransmissions.

Table

2
summarises various packet statistics across the test cases in
Table

1
:

•

For correct implementations (first, third, and fifth columns), monitors reject no packets.

•

For faulty implementations (second, fourth, and sixth columns), monitors reject packets.

•

With correct programs over TCP on unreliable networks (fifth column), monitors record retransmissions without introducing false rejects.

The statistics support the effectiveness of
NEST
against question

(Q2)
(correct monitoring) and

(Q3)
(monitoring of parallel sessions).
Table

2
also shows that when TCP is used, packet
retransmissions can happen even if the intended protocol is correctly
implemented – both on reliable and unreliable networks. Retransmission may occur when packets are lost (in unreliable netorks), or
depending on their delivery speed. E.g., the sender’s TCP stack may retransmit
a packet if an ACK does not arrive quickly enough, or the recipient’s TCP
buffer may become full and drop some packets, causing their retransmission.
NEST
correctly handles these situations.

6
Related Work

Session types have been extensively developed as standalone programming
languages
[
28
,
27
,
20
,
72
,
37
]
, and as libraries or tools
for existing languages such as Rust
[
38
,
42
,
17
,
16
,
15
]
,
Java
[
29
,
30
]
, Scala
[
65
]
, OCaml
[
59
,
32
]
,
Haskell
[
61
,
33
,
48
,
57
]
, Go
[
14
,
56
]
, and
others
[
75
]
.

Techniques for enforcing session types with run-time monitors have also been
studied in prior work, e.g.
[
11
,
12
,
8
,
9
,
54
,
18
,
55
]
.
These approaches focus on
application-layer
monitors abstracted from
the underlying network.
Burlò et al.
[
11
,
12
]
study binary session types where at least one party is a closed-box process (i.e., not statically verified). They synthesize Scala monitors, prove correctness guarantees, and establish the impossibility of sound and complete black-box monitoring.
Bocchi et al.
[
8
,
9
]
developed a monitored-network framework based on
\pi
-calculus processes and multiparty session types. Their “networks” are at a different layer from ours: they model a global routing application (akin to a message broker), implemented with AMQP
[
1
,
18
]
. That model allows unbounded buffering, so their semantics are close to our session types with queues (
Definition

3
) and do not address the synthesis requirements in
Section

4.3
that motivate
Definition

10
.

There is also growing work on run-time enforcement of network properties without session types. For example,

Hydra

[
62
]
deploys “checkers” on P4 switches
that enforce network-wide properties. These properties are expressed in terms
of packet trajectories through the network and observations of intermediate
state at each hop.
FLM

[
40
]
is a language and compiler
for enforcing line-rate network monitoring using programmable switches. Our
work is complementary: both
Hydra
and
FLM
could serve as
implementation platforms for the run-time monitors we propose.
At the microservice level, Grewal, Godfrey, and Hsu use run-time monitors to enforce policies
[
24
]
. Their goals are similar, but the technical setting differs: they rely on Istio Envoy proxies
[
35
]
on end hosts, whereas we target lower-level P4 devices. They also use declarative tree policies, while we use multiparty session types; studying whether a tree-policy-like formalism could model and monitor session protocols is an interesting direction for future work.

Giallorenzo et al.
[
23
]
propose
choreographic programming for specifying the global coordination between
Cloud-native Network Functions (CNF) architecture components. The
choreographic program is projected (i.e., compiled) into executable Java
programs that perform Virtual Network Functions (VNF) such as intrusion
detection and traffic filtering. They present a case study where a P4-enabled
virtual switch (based on
BMv2

[
58
]
, also adopted in our
evaluation) directs network traffic to the projected VNFs for network traffic
monitoring. The work
[
23
]
is orthogonal
to ours: they introduce a high-level software-defined network programming
architecture and do not address the problem of tracking session protocols;
moreover, their VNFs can implement and run arbitrary code without the
constraints of P4-enabled devices (which are a major factor in our work). In
principle, the P4 monitors generated by NEST could be deployed in the CNF
architecture of
[
23
]
to perform session
monitoring – and their VNFs could deploy and control NEST monitors via
P4Runtime
[
71
]
. A question that links our work to theirs is: is it
possible to synthesise NEST-style MAT tables from a choreographic program that
describes a network-level monitoring policy? This would allow moving the
monitoring and filtering logic from their (Java-based) VNFs to P4 devices.
This is intriguing and non-trivial work that would require bridging the wide
expressiveness gap between choreographic programming languages and P4.

7
Conclusion and Future Work

Conclusion.

In this work we addressed the challenge of enforcing session types directly in the network.
We developed a formal model of session-type-based monitors, synthesized
network-level monitors, and proved correctness under suitable network assumptions.
We then designed and implemented
NEST
, which generates (1) session-type monitors for P4-enabled switches and (2) APIs for writing communicating programs tracked by these monitors.
Across diverse settings and protocols, our evaluation shows accurate blocking of incorrect messages while allowing correct ones, with low network overhead.

To our knowledge, this is the first work to leverage session types to
implement network-level monitors for application-level properties.
NEST
demonstrates that it is possible to automatically synthesize network-level monitors from application-level protocols and deploy these on programmable network switches. Our results have limitations, in part due to the restrictions of P4 – e.g., the fact that the input protocols must be finite-state and half-duplex; still, we demonstrate that even with these restrictions, network-level session monitoring can support complex multiparty protocols.

Future Work.

Although our work is a first step toward network monitoring based on session types, several theoretical and practical challenges remain.

Towards monitoring completeness.

A natural next step is the dual of soundness (
Theorem

18
):
completeness
, i.e., rejection of all bad messages.
Proving completeness requires a precise characterization of “bad” messages (see
Examples

7
and
11
), and
[
11
, Theorem 21]
suggests that sound and complete monitoring may be unattainable in our setting.
Instead, we conjecture that our monitors are maximally strict: for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}
, if any accepting transition of
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
is turned into
reject
, then there exists a network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
that satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem

18
but not its thesis.

Formalising TCP-oriented monitors
. Our formal model (
Section

4
) focuses on the “core logic” of
network-level session monitoring in an idealised network with perfect message
delivery. This abstraction allows us to highlight the differences between our
network-level monitors and previous work on application-level session
monitoring

[
8
,
9
,
18
]
; extending
our formal model to cover TCP-oriented monitors under message duplication,
loss, and reordering is valuable and challenging future work. It would require
developing a (partial) formalisation of TCP, which is a significant
undertaking worth a separate paper, as evidenced by previous work in this area
(e.g.,

[
49
,
7
]
).
Therefore, we chose to focus our formalisation on the core monitor logic and
empirically validate the TCP-oriented extension outlined in

Section

3.3
.

Ensuring properties of
NEST
’s input session types.
As mentioned in
Footnote

1
and

Remark

19
, the current version of

NEST
assumes that the local session types given as input are part of a
multiparty protocol that is output-live (
Definition

15
) and half-duplex.

NEST
can be extended to check and guarantee these properties, e.g., via
bounded model checking, or by interfacing to existing tools (such as Scribble,

\nu
Scr, mpstk) to project local session types out of global types. This extension would make
NEST
more user-friendly without impacting its core functionality (i.e., monitor synthesis)
and the contributions of this work.

Encryption.
End-to-end encryption below the session header is compatible with
NEST
.
However, the current version assumes headers down to the session header are unencrypted, which may leak information.
Supporting encryption of packet and session headers is future work, potentially building on P4 encrypted-protocol techniques
[
25
,
26
]
and homomorphic encryption
[
21
]
.

Packet fragmentation.

Another direction concerns packet fragmentation.
The current version of
NEST
assumes a one-to-one correspondence
between session-type messages and network packets. This is often acceptable in modern IP networks with consistent MTUs, but application-level messages can still span multiple packets.

NEST
and our session API (
Section

3.4
) could
be extended to support messages spanning multiple packets, while still
avoiding network-level fragmentation, by adding a sequence number or
flag to the session header (
Section

3.1
) to
indicate whether the current message continues in the next packet.

Supporting other protocols and consistency models.

Finally, we plan to broaden our support for diverse transport and consistency requirements.
Our TCP-oriented monitor implementation should adapt to other reliable ordered transports, such as
QUIC

[
36
]
and
SCTP

[
41
]
.
Beyond transport, we aim to extend
NEST
to enforce network-level consistency models. Recent work has fruitfully connected session types with consistency guarantees such as causal consistency
[
50
]
and linearizability
[
69
]
. In parallel, other work has shown how to formally enforce a range of consistency models for programmable network
[
73
,
76
,
2
]
.

Appendix

Appendix A
Formal Model: Additional Details and Proofs

Example 21
.

Consider the session type: (for brevity, we omit the payload types)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}\;=\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{c}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\end{array}\right.\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\end{array}\right.

By
Definition

10
, the corresponding monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
can
immediately accept not only the two top-level outputs to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, but also
the inputs from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
— which appear later in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, but may be
delivered earlier by the surrounding network. For the top-level outputs, we have (by rule
STMon-IntC
in
Definition

10
):

{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{c}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}\end{array}\right.}}\right\rrbracket}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}}}\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}}}

Notice that the choice of message sent to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
restricts what the
monitor will accept from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
afterwards.
The monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}
can also immediately accept the inputs from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
by rule
STMon-IntPfx
in
Definition

10
. The transition
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{c}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}

is fired by the following derivation:

\small\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\Rule{}
{
{\transition{\enc{\roleQ\stExtC\left\{\begin{array}{@{}l@{}}
\stLabFmt{c} \stSeq\stEnd\\
\stLabFmt{d} \stSeq\stEnd\end{array}\right.}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{c}}{}}}
{\enc{\stEnd}}}
}
{STMon-ExtC}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stT}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{c}}{}}}
{\enc{\roleP\stIntC\stLabFmt{a} \stSeq\stEnd}}}\ \textsc{STMon-IntPfx}

Instead, the transition
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}}\,\checkmark}

is fired by the following derivation:

\small\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}
\Rule{}
{
{\transition{\enc{\roleQ\stExtC\left\{\begin{array}{@{}l@{}}
\stLabFmt{c} \stSeq\stEnd\\
\stLabFmt{d} \stSeq\stEnd\end{array}\right.}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{d}}{}}}
{\enc{\stEnd}}}
}
{STMon-ExtC}
\quad\Rule{}
{
{\transition{\enc{\roleQ\stExtC\stLabFmt{d} \stSeq\stEnd}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{d}}{}}}
{\enc{\stEnd}}}
}
{STMon-ExtC}
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\transition{\enc{\stT}}
{\monLabYes{\stEnqueueTag{\roleQ}{\stLabFmt{d}}{}}}
{\enc{\roleP\stIntC\left\{\begin{array}{@{}l@{}}
\stLabFmt{a} \stSeq\stEnd\\\stLabFmt{b} \stSeq\stEnd\end{array}\right.}}}\ \textsc{STMon-IntPfx}

Observe that in the first case, the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{c}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}

causes rule
STMon-IntPfx
to “prune” the session type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
in the monitor state, because in its internal choice there is only one
branch in which the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{c}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can be received.
After that, the monitor only accepts sending message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
to

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, as it is the only output compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
Instead, in the second case, the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
causes
rule
STMon-IntPfx
to keep both branches of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
in the monitor
state: this is because in both branches the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{d}}
from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
can be received. After that, the monitor still allows sending
either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, as both options are
compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

This strategy for accepting messages is necessary
because, depending on the overall multiparty interaction, messages from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
may be delivered before those to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
are sent. This phenomenon is further
illustrated in
Example

12
below.

\blacklozenge

Definition 22

(Half-Duplex Network)
.

Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
if queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
contains message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
.
We say a network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is
half-duplex
if, whenever

{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau}}{}^{\!\!*}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\mathop{\parallel}\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}:{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i}}}})}}}}
,
then for all
i,j\in I
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{j}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i}}

(for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
)
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}}})}\not\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{j}}

(for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S^{\prime}}
).

\lozenge

Example 23

(On the half-duplex restriction)
.

Consider the following session types:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}\qquad{and}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}^{\prime}}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}^{\prime}}}}

Consider the following simple unmonitored network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
with the two
session types above plus input queues, for the end host roles
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}\;\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}

This network executes with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
sending each other the
messages
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}
, respectively. The messages are
delivered to the input queues of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, reaching the network
configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
below:

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}}\;\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{a}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{b}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}}}}

Then, the session types of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
consume the respective input messages and empty the respective queues,
looping back to the network configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
.

Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is output-live (by
Definition

15
) but is
not

half-duplex (by
Definition

22
), because it can reach the
configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
above where the input queue of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
contains a
message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and
vice versa
.

Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
above does
not
satisfy the hypotheses of

Theorem

18
. And indeed, consider the instrumented version of

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, by
Definition

14
:

{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}}\;\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\parallel}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}}

By
Definition

10
, the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
only
accepts an outgoing message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and rejects any incoming message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
; and similarly,

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
only accepts an outgoing message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and rejects any incoming message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. Therefore, the monitored network
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
cannot
advance to a configuration corresponding to
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
above.

In principle, one may attempt lifting the half-duplex restriction of
Theorem

18
(and thus, covering the example above)
by allowing our monitors to accept inputs coming from a role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
even if such inputs are expected after an output to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. To this end,
Definition

10
may be relaxed by extending
rules
STMon-IntPfx
and
STMon-ExtPfx

to “skip” internal choices from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

However, this extension to
Definition

10
, would cause a
problem: the monitor
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
would become infinite-state, by
accepting unbounded sequences of inputs from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, similarly to

Example

13
. The same issue would affect the monitor

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
. In general, this relaxed version of

Definition

10
would cause most non-trivial session types with
recursion (including many examples we evaluate in
Section

5
)
to generate infinite-state monitors that would not be representable in
finite P4 tables by
NEST
(
Section

3
).

\blacklozenge

A.1
Proofs

Definition 24

(Auxiliary notation)
.

Given a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
, we define its
unfolding

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\!\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right\}}}\right)}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
otherwise.

\lozenge

Proposition 25
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is output-live and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau}}{}^{\!\!*}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}

is output-live.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
[
22
, Prop. 4.9]
,
but using our output-liveness (
Definition

15
) instead of their
liveness
[
22
, Def. 4.7]
.
∎

Lemma 26
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}^{\prime}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,

Proof.

We first prove that if two queues
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
are equal except
for the order of two consecutive messages having different senders, then

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime\prime}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
implies

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.

We then prove the main statement by observing that, by hypothesis and

Definition

3
, there is a sequence of
n
applications of

\equiv
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{0}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{1}}\equiv\ldots\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{n}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}
, and for all
i\in 1..n-1
, the queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i}}
is
equal to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i+1}}
except for the order of two consecutive messages having
different senders. Therefore, for
i\in 1..n-1
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
implies

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma_{i+1}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
(by the result above),
and we obtain the thesis.
∎

Corollary 27
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
is defined, then

{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
.

Proof.

Consequence of
Lemma

26
and
Definition

10
.
∎

Lemma 28
.

Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\right)}
is defined. Then, we have that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}

implies

{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\right)}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}\right)}}
.

Proof.

By inversion of rule
Net-Dequeue
in
Definition

6

we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}
, and thus:

1.

By inversion of rule
SQ-Dequeue
in
Definition

3
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}
such that, for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}
.

2.

Therefore, by rule
SQ-Dequeue
in

Definition

3
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}
.

3.

By

Item

1
and

Definition

3
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}
must be an external choice from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
including a message with
label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
. Thus, by

Definition

10
, we have

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}

(using rule
STMon-Rec
to unfold
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
if needed, and then

STMon-ExtC
).

4.

By

Item

1
,

Item

2
,

Item

3
,

Definition

10
, and

Lemma

26
,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}\right\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}
.

5.

Hence, by rule
M-Dequeue
in
Definition

8

and
Item

4
,
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}
.

6.

Thus, by

Item

5
and rule

MNet-Dequeue
in
Definition

9
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}_{1}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{i}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}}}}
.

Therefore, by
Item

6

and
Definition

14

we obtain the thesis.
∎

Lemma 29
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\right)}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime}}}
, then

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}\right)}
.

Proof.

By inversion of rule
MNet-Dequeue
in
Definition

9
, rule
M-Dequeue
in
Definition

8
, and rule
SQ-Dequeue
in
Definition

3
we infer the same shape of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}
obtained in the proof of

Lemma

28
above, and thus, the same transition

{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}
. Then, we conclude
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}\right)}

by
Definition

14
.∎

Proposition 30
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}
,
then

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}}
.

Proof.

By inversion of rule
SQ-Send
in
Definition

3
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}
must be an internal choice that sends a message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with label

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
. Then, the result is direct consequence of
rules
STMon-IntC
(and possibly
STMon-Rec
)
in
Definition

10
,
and
Definition

8
.
∎

Lemma 31
.

For any sequence of input/output labels
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}
implies

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.

Proof.

From
Definition

10
we have that for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}

implies
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.
We then prove the thesis by induction on
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

Immediate by
Definition

10
.
∎

Lemma 33
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,
then there is a non-empty set
\mathcal{F}
containing all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})

where
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
is a (possibly empty) sequence of input/output labels not involving
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

such that
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{\!\!}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
. Moreover, for all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})\in\mathcal{F}
, we have:

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
, and

2.

{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}\,\checkmark}}}{\!\!}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.

Proof.

To prove the first part of the statement, we construct
\mathcal{F}
as:

\mathcal{F}\;=\;\left\{(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})\;\middle|\;\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}\text{ does not involve ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ and }{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{\!\!}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}\right\}

Observe that
\mathcal{F}
above is not empty: this is because, by hypothesis and
Definition

10
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
must contain some external choices containing message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and payload
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
,
which can be fired by the axiom
STMon-ExtC
in a derivation based on rules
STMon-Rec
,
STMon-IntPfx
,
STMon-ExtPfx
.
More precisely, this means that there exists and indexing set
K\neq\emptyset

such that each of the aforementioned external choices in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
has the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{{{{\&}}}}_{i\in I_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}
for
k\in K
,
such that
\forall k\in K:\exists i\in I_{k}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
. We construct each
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
in the definition of
\mathcal{F}
above by induction on the derivation that proves the hypothesis
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,
obtaining one transition in
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
for each application of rules
STMon-IntPfx
and
STMon-ExtPfx
(which, by
Definition

10
, does not involve
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
);
let us call that derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}
.

For the
“moreover…”
part of the statement, given
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
we obtain item
1

by induction the derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}
above, turning each application of rules
STMon-IntPfx
and
STMon-ExtPfx
into an application of
S-IntC
and
S-ExtC
, from
Definition

3
, respectively.

Finally, item
2
is direct consequence of
Lemma

31
.
∎

Lemma 34
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
, then
there is a non-empty set
\mathcal{F}
containing all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})

where
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
is a sequence of input/output labels including inputs for all
messages in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
maintaining sender ordering and ending with one of such
inputs,
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
. Moreover, for all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})\in\mathcal{F}
, we have:

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
, and

2.

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.

Proof.

We construct
\mathcal{F}
by induction on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
. The base case (i.e., when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
is empty) is immediate.
In the inductive case
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}

for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.
By the i.h. we have a set
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
containing all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}})

where
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}
is a sequence of input/output labels including inputs for all
messages in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}

such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}
; moreover,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
. We now construct
\mathcal{F}
from
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, by inspecting each pair

(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and determining whether we can select
and extend the pair to include new actions ensuring that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
is fired and a desired
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
is reached.

Recalling that by hypothesis we have
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
,
we apply
Lemma

33
to obtain a non-empty set
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
containing all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}})

where
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}
is a (possibly empty) sequence of input/output labels not involving
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

such that
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}}}{\!\!}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}
. Moreover, for all pairs
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}
and

{{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}\,\checkmark}}}{\!\!}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.
We construct the desired set
\mathcal{F}
as follows:

\mathcal{F}\;=\;\left\{(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\exists(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}},\_)\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:\exists(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}:\\
(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}})\;\text{ is a prefix of }\;\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}\\
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}=\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}\cdot(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}\setminus\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}})\end{array}\right\}

i.e., from all the input/output sequences
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}
in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, we
only select those
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}
that from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}
can lead to performing the action

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
(since
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma^{\prime}}
is a prefix of some
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}

in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
, which contains all sequences from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime\prime}}
that
eventually perform
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
as first input from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
); then, we concatenate
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime}}}
and
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho^{\prime\prime}}}
(avoiding the duplication of
the common input/output actions), thus obtaining a
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}
that performs
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
and
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\rho}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}}
.

For the
“moreover…”
part of the statement, we obtain item
1

from the construction of
\mathcal{F}
above, while item
2
is direct consequence of
Lemma

31
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is an output-live, half-duplex network such that

{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}
, containing
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}
.
Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
is defined. Then,
{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\lceil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\right\rceil}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}\;\text{ contains }\;{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}}}}

(by hypothesis and inversion of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}
)

(1)

Also by hypothesis,
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
is defined, i.e.,

\displaystyle\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}}\right\rrbracket}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}

(by hypothesis and
Definition

10
)

(2)

Notice that, by
Definition

3
(rule
SQ-Recv
),
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}}}
.
Therefore, by rule
MNet-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
-Good
of
Definition

9
,
we can prove the statement by proving:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}

(3)

By contradiction, assume that (
3
) does
not
hold,
and thus,
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}}

implies
{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathbin{{\langle}\!{|}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}
.
By
Definition

10
the quantification “
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}\ldots
” above must be vacuous,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\not\exists{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}}\right\rrbracket}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}\,\checkmark}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.21,0.5,0.1}M^{\prime}}}

(by
Definition

10
)

(4)

By
Definition

10
,
eq.

4

has two possible implications:

1.

All the external choices from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
that are reached first in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}

cannot syntactically consume the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}

from the input queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
. Observe that, by
(
2
) and

Lemma

34
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
consumes its input messages

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
by reducing into a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
reachable from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}

— and therefore, such
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
also does not syntactically contain any
external choice from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
that can consume the message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
which is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s input queue in

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
, by
(
1
). This
means that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
is
not
output-live (by
Definition

15
), and
therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is not output-live either (by the contrapositive of

Proposition

25
) — contradiction.

2.

There are external choices in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}
that could consume the message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
, but they are prefixed by an internal
choice towards
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
. Observe that, by
(
2
) and

Lemma

34
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
consumes its input messages

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
by reducing into a session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
reachable from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}

by firing the same inputs/outputs of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}
(plus the inputs from

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sigma}
) in the same order. Therefore, either before or after reducing to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
must emit an output to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
before it can consume
the message
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s input queue.
But then, by rule
Net-Comm
in

Definition

6
, a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
will
land in the input queue of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
, while the message

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathbin{\!\mathbf{\triangleright}\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})}
is still in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s input queue.
Therefore, by
Definition

22
we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
is not
half-duplex — contradiction.

We have thus proven that if we negate
(
3
) we contradict at
least one hypothesis in the statement. Therefore,
(
3
) must hold, and this
(by rule
MNet-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\gamma}
-Good
of
Definition

9
)
leads to the thesis.
∎

See
18

Proof.

Take any output-live and half-duplex network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, and consider the following relation:

{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}\;=\;\left\{\left({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right)}\right)\;\middle|\;{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau}}{}^{\!\!*}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right\}

(5)

We now prove that
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
is an internal bisimulation. To this end, we
inspect each pair
({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime\prime}})\in{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
, and we show that the pair
satisfies clauses
1

and
2
of
Definition

17
. By
Equation

5
, the pair has the form

({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right)})
, and we have the following cases.

•

\exists{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}
. We must prove that clause
1
of
Definition

17
is satisfied.

By inversion of rule
Net-Par
in
Definition

6

we have
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}}}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}}
;
moreover, by
Net-Par
in
Definition

6
,

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\ddot{T}^{\prime}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

(8)

Hence, we obtain:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right)}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}\right)}}

(by (
7
), (
8
),
and
MNet-Comm
in
Definition

9
)

(9)

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}\right)})\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}

(by (
9
) and (
5
))

as required by clause
1
of
Definition

17
.

•

\exists{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime}}^{\prime}i:{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right)}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime\prime}}}
. We must prove that clause
2
of
Definition

17
is satisfied.

We infer the shape of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
(which involves a communication of a
message with label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
from an internal
choice in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
towards
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, similarly to the previous case)
and then show that there is an unmonitored network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}\tau({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S})})}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}

(i.e., the unmonitored
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}
can perform the same communication allowed by
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime}}}\right)}
)
and
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\widehat{N}^{\prime\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}\right)}
. Therefore, since by (
5
) we have
({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N^{\prime\prime}}}\right)})\in\mathop{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
,
we have satisfied clause
2
of
Definition

17
.

We thus proven that, for any output-live network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, there exists a relation

\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
(
Equation

5
) which is an internal
bisimulation (by
Definition

17
); moreover, we have
({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N},{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)})\in{\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}}
(by
Equation

5
). Therefore, we conclude that, for any output-live network
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}\mathrel{\overset{\tau}{\sim}}{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.33,0.13}\operatorname{mon}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.4,0.8}N}}\right)}
(by
Definition

17
).
∎∎

B.10
